\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Posterior draws are not new data}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A posterior draw is not a new student survey. It is not a possible
future dataset.

A posterior draw is a simulated plausible value of the unknown
parameter, given the prior and the data we observed.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Summarising posterior draws}\label{summarising-posterior-draws}

Once we have posterior draws, many Bayesian summaries become
straightforward.

We can estimate the posterior mean:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E(\theta \mid X)
&\approx \text{mean of posterior draws}.
\end{aligned}
\]

We can estimate a \(95\%\) credible interval:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{95\% credible interval}
&\approx \text{middle 95\% of posterior draws}.
\end{aligned}
\]

We can estimate posterior probabilities:

\[
\begin{aligned}
P(\theta > 0.60 \mid X)
&\approx \text{proportion of posterior draws greater than }0.60.
\end{aligned}
\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{posterior\_summary }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ posterior\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior median}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{median}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{2.5\% quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(theta, }\FloatTok{0.025}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{97.5\% quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(theta, }\FloatTok{0.975}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{P(theta \textgreater{} 0.60)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(theta }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.60}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{P(theta \textgreater{} 0.70)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(theta }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.70}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{everything}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Value"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{posterior\_summary }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{Value =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(Value, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.8\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lr}
\toprule
Summary & Value \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Posterior mean & 0.636 \\ 
Posterior median & 0.638 \\ 
2.5\% quantile & 0.493 \\ 
97.5\% quantile & 0.769 \\ 
P(theta > 0.60) & 0.697 \\ 
P(theta > 0.70) & 0.193 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The interpretation is direct. If \(P(\theta > 0.60 \mid X)\) is large,
then after seeing the survey data, there is strong posterior evidence
that more than \(60\%\) of students would use the bus.

A useful visual habit is to shade posterior probabilities. For example,
suppose the university thinks the service is worth serious consideration
if more than \(60\%\) of students would use it.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{posterior\_density }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{theta =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.999}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{density =} \FunctionTok{dbeta}\NormalTok{(theta, alpha\_post, beta\_post),}
  \AttributeTok{region =} \FunctionTok{if\_else}\NormalTok{(theta }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.60}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"theta \textgreater{} 0.60"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"theta \textless{}= 0.60"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{posterior\_density }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ density, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ region, }\AttributeTok{fill =}\NormalTok{ region)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_area}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_vline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{xintercept =} \FloatTok{0.60}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{linetype =} \StringTok{"dashed"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Posterior density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{fill =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Posterior probability that the true proportion is above 0.60"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-6-1.pdf}}

This is one of the most useful features of Bayesian inference. We can
ask questions about the unknown parameter directly.

\begin{quote}
What is the probability that the true proportion is above \(0.60\),
given the data?
\end{quote}

In a Bayesian analysis, that question is natural.

\section{Simulation error}\label{simulation-error}

Posterior simulation is powerful, but it is still simulation.

If we simulate \(100\) posterior draws, our summaries will be a little
rough. If we simulate \(10{,}000\) draws, they will usually be more
stable. The exact posterior has not changed. Only our simulation-based
approximation has improved.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2420}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{simulation\_sizes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{simulation\_error }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{map\_dfr}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{  simulation\_sizes,}
  \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(m) \{}
\NormalTok{    draws }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rbeta}\NormalTok{(m, alpha\_post, beta\_post)}
    \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
      \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Number of posterior draws}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ m,}
      \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior mean estimate}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(draws),}
      \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{P(theta \textgreater{} 0.60) estimate}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(draws }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.60}\NormalTok{),}
      \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Lower credible limit}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(draws, }\FloatTok{0.025}\NormalTok{),}
      \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Upper credible limit}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(draws, }\FloatTok{0.975}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{  \}}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{simulation\_error }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{across}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{where}\NormalTok{(is.numeric), }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(.x, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{))) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{1\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}rrrrr}
\toprule
Number of posterior draws & Posterior mean estimate & P(theta > 0.60) estimate & Lower credible limit & Upper credible limit \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
100 & 0.645 & 0.700 & 0.485 & 0.774 \\ 
500 & 0.634 & 0.678 & 0.487 & 0.770 \\ 
2000 & 0.635 & 0.692 & 0.494 & 0.768 \\ 
10000 & 0.637 & 0.701 & 0.493 & 0.772 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The estimates should become more stable as the number of draws
increases. This is called \textbf{Monte Carlo error}. It is the small
amount of numerical error caused by using a finite number of simulation
draws.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Monte Carlo error}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

Monte Carlo error is not statistical uncertainty about \(\theta\).

It is uncertainty caused by approximating a posterior summary using a
finite number of simulated draws.

We can reduce Monte Carlo error by using more posterior draws.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Why conjugacy is not enough}\label{why-conjugacy-is-not-enough}

The beta-binomial example is convenient because the posterior has a
known form.

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Beta prior} \times \text{Binomial likelihood}
&\rightarrow \text{Beta posterior}.
\end{aligned}
\]

This is called conjugacy.

Conjugate models are useful for learning because they let us see the
logic clearly. But many real statistical models are not conjugate, or
become inconvenient once we add more realistic details.

Suppose we want to model late-night bus demand, but we believe demand
depends on:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  whether students live on campus or commute;
\item
  whether they have an exam the next morning;
\item
  whether it is raining;
\item
  whether they usually study late;
\item
  whether they live near a train station;
\item
  whether the survey was taken during SWOTVAC or during semester.
\end{itemize}

Now the model may have many parameters. The posterior may not have a
tidy formula.

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{1\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}llll}
\toprule
Situation & Example & Posterior form & What we can do \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Simple conjugate model & One unknown proportion with a beta prior & Known distribution & Use algebra or direct simulation from the known posterior \\ 
More realistic model & Demand depends on several student characteristics & Often not available in a simple formula & Approximate the posterior using computational methods \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

This is where simulation becomes more than a convenience. It becomes a
way of doing Bayesian inference when algebra is not enough.

Before we get to Markov chain Monte Carlo, we will start with the
simplest computational idea: grid approximation.

\section{Grid approximation}\label{grid-approximation}

A grid approximation is a brute-force way to approximate a posterior
distribution.

The idea is simple.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Choose many possible values of the parameter.
\item
  Calculate the prior density at each value.
\item
  Calculate the likelihood at each value.
\item
  Multiply prior by likelihood.
\item
  Normalise the result so the weights add to \(1\).
\end{enumerate}

This gives a discrete approximation to the posterior.

Let us return to the bus survey.

We use the same model:

\[
\begin{aligned}
X \mid \theta
&\sim \text{Binomial}(40, \theta), \\
\theta
&\sim \text{Beta}(2, 2).
\end{aligned}
\]

Instead of using the conjugate formula, we create a grid of possible
\(\theta\) values.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{grid\_posterior }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{theta =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.999}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{prior =} \FunctionTok{dbeta}\NormalTok{(theta, alpha\_prior, beta\_prior),}
    \AttributeTok{likelihood =} \FunctionTok{dbinom}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{size =}\NormalTok{ n, }\AttributeTok{prob =}\NormalTok{ theta),}
    \AttributeTok{unnormalised\_posterior =}\NormalTok{ prior }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ likelihood,}
    \AttributeTok{posterior\_weight =}\NormalTok{ unnormalised\_posterior }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(unnormalised\_posterior)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{grid\_posterior }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(theta, prior, likelihood, unnormalised\_posterior, posterior\_weight) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{slice}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \DecValTok{150}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{across}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{where}\NormalTok{(is.numeric), }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(.x, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{))) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{1\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}rrrrr}
\toprule
theta & prior & likelihood & unnormalised\_posterior & posterior\_weight \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
0.001000 & 0.005994 & 0.000000 & 0.000000 & 0.000000 \\ 
0.150850 & 0.768565 & 0.000000 & 0.000000 & 0.000000 \\ 
0.300700 & 1.261676 & 0.000004 & 0.000005 & 0.000000 \\ 
0.450550 & 1.485328 & 0.005271 & 0.007830 & 0.000238 \\ 
0.600399 & 1.439520 & 0.106618 & 0.153479 & 0.004672 \\ 
0.750249 & 1.124252 & 0.048535 & 0.054566 & 0.001661 \\ 
0.900099 & 0.539524 & 0.000015 & 0.000008 & 0.000000 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The posterior weights are tiny because there are many grid points. What
matters is their relative size. Values of \(\theta\) that make the prior
and likelihood product large receive more posterior weight.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{grid\_long }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ grid\_posterior }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{transmute}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    theta,}
    \AttributeTok{Prior =}\NormalTok{ prior }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(prior),}
    \AttributeTok{Likelihood =}\NormalTok{ likelihood }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(likelihood),}
    \AttributeTok{Posterior =}\NormalTok{ posterior\_weight }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(posterior\_weight)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(Prior, Likelihood, Posterior),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"quantity"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"scaled\_value"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{grid\_long }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ scaled\_value, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ quantity)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Scaled value"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Grid approximation: prior, likelihood, and posterior"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Each curve is scaled to have maximum value 1 so the shapes can be compared"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-10-1.pdf}}

The posterior sits between the prior and the likelihood. It is not
simply an average of the two curves, but it reflects both. The prior
says what was plausible before the data. The likelihood says what the
survey data support. The posterior combines them.

\subsection{Sampling from the grid}\label{sampling-from-the-grid}

Once we have grid weights, we can sample from them.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2420}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{grid\_draws }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{draw =} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{theta =} \FunctionTok{sample}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    grid\_posterior}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{theta,}
    \AttributeTok{size =} \DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{replace =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{prob =}\NormalTok{ grid\_posterior}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{posterior\_weight}
\NormalTok{  )}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{grid\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{after\_stat}\NormalTok{(density), }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Grid posterior draws"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{bins =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Posterior draws from the grid approximation"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{guides}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{color =} \FunctionTok{guide\_legend}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{override.aes =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{fill =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-11-1.pdf}}

We can compare the grid approximation with the exact beta posterior.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{comparison\_density }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{theta =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.999}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{),}
  \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Exact Beta posterior}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{dbeta}\NormalTok{(theta, alpha\_post, beta\_post),}
  \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Grid approximation}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ grid\_posterior}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{posterior\_weight }\SpecialCharTok{/}
    \FunctionTok{diff}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{range}\NormalTok{(grid\_posterior}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{theta)) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(grid\_posterior}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{theta)}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Exact Beta posterior}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Grid approximation}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"method"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"density"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{comparison\_density }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ density, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ method)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"The grid approximation closely matches the exact posterior"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-12-1.pdf}}

The grid method is deliberately simple. It is also important because it
makes Bayesian computation visible.

We did not need a magic formula. We used the central Bayesian
relationship:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{posterior}
&\propto \text{prior} \times \text{likelihood}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Then we normalised the result.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Grid approximation is Bayes by calculation}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

Grid approximation is not a different kind of Bayesian inference.

It is the same Bayesian idea implemented by direct calculation over many
possible parameter values.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Why grids do not scale well}\label{why-grids-do-not-scale-well}

For one unknown parameter, a grid is easy.

For two unknown parameters, we need a two-dimensional grid. For three, a
three-dimensional grid. The number of grid points grows quickly.

Suppose we use \(100\) possible values for each unknown parameter.

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.7\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}rrr}
\toprule
Number of parameters & Grid points per parameter & Total grid points \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
1 & 100 & 100 \\ 
2 & 100 & 10,000 \\ 
3 & 100 & 1,000,000 \\ 
4 & 100 & 100,000,000 \\ 
5 & 100 & 10,000,000,000 \\ 
6 & 100 & 1,000,000,000,000 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

With six parameters, a \(100\)-point grid in each dimension would
require one trillion grid points. That is not a convenient calculation.

This is sometimes called the \textbf{curse of dimensionality}. It is one
reason Bayesian computation needs smarter simulation methods.

\section{The idea of MCMC}\label{the-idea-of-mcmc}

Markov chain Monte Carlo, usually shortened to \textbf{MCMC}, is a
family of methods for drawing samples from complicated posterior
distributions.

The name sounds intimidating, but the intuition is approachable.

Imagine a walker exploring a landscape. The height of the landscape
represents the posterior density. High places are parameter values that
are more plausible after seeing the data. Low places are parameter
values that are less plausible.

A good Bayesian simulation should spend more time in the high-posterior
regions and less time in the low-posterior regions.

MCMC does this by creating a sequence of draws:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\theta_1,\ \theta_2,\ \theta_3,\ \ldots,\ \theta_m.
\end{aligned}
\]

Each draw depends on the previous draw. That is why it is called a
chain.

The goal is for the long-run behaviour of the chain to match the
posterior distribution.

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{1\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}ll}
\toprule
Term & Meaning in this chapter \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Monte Carlo & Using simulation to approximate a quantity \\ 
Markov chain & A sequence where the next value depends on the current value \\ 
MCMC & A simulation method that constructs a chain whose long-run distribution is the posterior \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

We will not try to cover all of MCMC. Modern Bayesian software often
uses advanced algorithms such as Hamiltonian Monte Carlo. Here, we focus
on one simple algorithm: the random-walk Metropolis algorithm.

The aim is to understand the idea, not to become an expert in
computational Bayesian statistics.

\section{A gentle Metropolis
algorithm}\label{a-gentle-metropolis-algorithm}

The Metropolis algorithm works by proposing small moves and deciding
whether to accept them.

For the bus survey, the posterior density is proportional to:

\[
\begin{aligned}
f(\theta \mid x)
&\propto f(x \mid \theta)f(\theta) \\
&\propto \theta^x(1 - \theta)^{n - x}\theta^{\alpha - 1}(1 - \theta)^{\beta - 1}.
\end{aligned}
\]

For our example:

\[
\begin{aligned}
f(\theta \mid x)
&\propto \theta^{26}(1 - \theta)^{14}\theta^{1}(1 - \theta)^{1} \\
&\propto \theta^{27}(1 - \theta)^{15}.
\end{aligned}
\]

We do not actually need the normalising constant. This is one of the
useful features of Metropolis. We only need to compare posterior
heights.

The algorithm is:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Start at a possible value of \(\theta\).
\item
  Propose a nearby value \(\theta^*\).
\item
  Compare the posterior height at \(\theta^*\) with the posterior height
  at the current value.
\item
  If the proposal is better, accept it.
\item
  If the proposal is worse, sometimes accept it anyway.
\item
  Repeat many times.
\end{enumerate}

Accepting worse moves may sound strange. But it helps the chain explore
the full posterior rather than getting stuck at the highest point.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Why accept worse moves?}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

If the chain only accepted moves to higher posterior density, it would
climb to the mode and stay there.

A posterior distribution is not only about the best value. It is about
the whole range of plausible values.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log\_posterior }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(theta, x, n, alpha, beta) \{}
  \FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    theta }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{0} \SpecialCharTok{|}\NormalTok{ theta }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}
    \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{,}
    \FunctionTok{dbinom}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{size =}\NormalTok{ n, }\AttributeTok{prob =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
      \FunctionTok{dbeta}\NormalTok{(theta, alpha, beta, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  )}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{run\_metropolis }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n\_iter, start, proposal\_sd, x, n, alpha, beta) \{}
\NormalTok{  theta }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(n\_iter)}
\NormalTok{  accepted }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{logical}\NormalTok{(n\_iter)}
\NormalTok{  theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ start}

  \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n\_iter) \{}
\NormalTok{    proposal }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =}\NormalTok{ theta[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{sd =}\NormalTok{ proposal\_sd)}

\NormalTok{    log\_ratio }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_posterior}\NormalTok{(proposal, x, n, alpha, beta) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}
      \FunctionTok{log\_posterior}\NormalTok{(theta[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], x, n, alpha, beta)}

    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ log\_ratio) \{}
\NormalTok{      theta[i] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ proposal}
\NormalTok{      accepted[i] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ConstantTok{TRUE}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{      theta[i] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ theta[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{  \}}

  \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{iteration =} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n\_iter,}
    \AttributeTok{theta =}\NormalTok{ theta,}
    \AttributeTok{accepted =}\NormalTok{ accepted}
\NormalTok{  )}
\NormalTok{\}}

\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2420}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{metropolis\_draws }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{run\_metropolis}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{n\_iter =} \DecValTok{12000}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{start =} \FloatTok{0.30}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{proposal\_sd =} \FloatTok{0.06}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ x,}
  \AttributeTok{n =}\NormalTok{ n,}
  \AttributeTok{alpha =}\NormalTok{ alpha\_prior,}
  \AttributeTok{beta =}\NormalTok{ beta\_prior}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{stage =} \FunctionTok{if\_else}\NormalTok{(iteration }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Warm{-}up"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Kept draws"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{metropolis\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ iteration, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ stage)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Iteration"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"A Metropolis chain for the bus survey posterior"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Early warm{-}up draws are often discarded"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-15-1.pdf}}

The trace plot shows the chain wandering around the high-posterior
region. Early in the chain, the values may still reflect the starting
point. These early values are often called \textbf{warm-up} or
\textbf{burn-in} draws.

For this simple example, the chain quickly reaches the main posterior
region. In more complicated models, warm-up and convergence are more
serious issues.

Let us discard the first \(2000\) iterations and compare the remaining
draws with the exact posterior.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{metropolis\_kept }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ metropolis\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(iteration }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{exact\_density }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{theta =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.999}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{density =} \FunctionTok{dbeta}\NormalTok{(theta, alpha\_post, beta\_post)}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{metropolis\_kept }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{after\_stat}\NormalTok{(density), }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Metropolis draws"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{bins =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ exact\_density,}
    \FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ density, }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Exact posterior"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Metropolis draws approximate the posterior distribution"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{guides}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{color =} \FunctionTok{guide\_legend}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{override.aes =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{fill =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-16-1.pdf}}

The Metropolis draws are not independent in the same way as the direct
\texttt{rbeta()} draws were. Each draw depends on the previous draw. But
if the chain is working well, the collection of kept draws still
approximates the posterior distribution.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{metropolis\_summary }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ metropolis\_kept }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Acceptance rate}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(accepted),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior median}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{median}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{2.5\% quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(theta, }\FloatTok{0.025}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{97.5\% quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(theta, }\FloatTok{0.975}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{P(theta \textgreater{} 0.60)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(theta }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.60}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{everything}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Value"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{metropolis\_summary }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{Value =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(Value, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.8\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lr}
\toprule
Summary & Value \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Acceptance rate & 0.749 \\ 
Posterior mean & 0.638 \\ 
Posterior median & 0.640 \\ 
2.5\% quantile & 0.493 \\ 
97.5\% quantile & 0.774 \\ 
P(theta > 0.60) & 0.705 \\

\end{table}

The exact numbers will not be identical to the analytic posterior
summaries, because this is a simulation. But they should be close.

\section{What can go wrong with
MCMC?}\label{what-can-go-wrong-with-mcmc}

MCMC is powerful, but it is not magic. A chain can behave badly.

One common problem is that the proposed steps are too small. The chain
moves, but very slowly. It may take a long time to explore the
posterior.

Another problem is that the proposed steps are too large. The chain
proposes values that are often unreasonable, so many proposals are
rejected. The chain may stay in the same place for long stretches.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2420}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{chain\_small\_steps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{run\_metropolis}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{n\_iter =} \DecValTok{3000}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{start =} \FloatTok{0.30}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{proposal\_sd =} \FloatTok{0.005}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ x,}
  \AttributeTok{n =}\NormalTok{ n,}
  \AttributeTok{alpha =}\NormalTok{ alpha\_prior,}
  \AttributeTok{beta =}\NormalTok{ beta\_prior}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{chain =} \StringTok{"Proposal steps too small"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{chain\_large\_steps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{run\_metropolis}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{n\_iter =} \DecValTok{3000}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{start =} \FloatTok{0.30}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{proposal\_sd =} \FloatTok{0.50}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ x,}
  \AttributeTok{n =}\NormalTok{ n,}
  \AttributeTok{alpha =}\NormalTok{ alpha\_prior,}
  \AttributeTok{beta =}\NormalTok{ beta\_prior}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{chain =} \StringTok{"Proposal steps too large"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{chain\_good\_steps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{run\_metropolis}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{n\_iter =} \DecValTok{3000}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{start =} \FloatTok{0.30}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{proposal\_sd =} \FloatTok{0.06}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ x,}
  \AttributeTok{n =}\NormalTok{ n,}
  \AttributeTok{alpha =}\NormalTok{ alpha\_prior,}
  \AttributeTok{beta =}\NormalTok{ beta\_prior}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{chain =} \StringTok{"Reasonable proposal steps"}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{bind\_rows}\NormalTok{(chain\_small\_steps, chain\_good\_steps, chain\_large\_steps) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ iteration, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ chain)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{facet\_wrap}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ chain, }\AttributeTok{ncol =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Iteration"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Different proposal sizes can produce very different chains"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"none"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-18-1.pdf}}

The trace plot is one of the first things to inspect in an MCMC
analysis.

A healthy trace plot usually looks like a fuzzy caterpillar: it moves
around a stable region without obvious trends, long flat sections, or
permanent drifting.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{A useful MCMC habit}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

Do not only look at the final posterior summary table.

Always ask:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Did the chain explore the posterior?
\item
  Did it get stuck?
\item
  Did it depend too much on the starting value?
\item
  Would another chain started somewhere else give a similar answer?
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

In professional Bayesian modelling, convergence diagnostics are
essential. In this chapter, the main point is simpler:

\begin{quote}
MCMC gives approximate posterior draws, but we should check whether the
simulation behaved sensibly.
\end{quote}

\section{Posterior prediction}\label{posterior-prediction}

So far, we have focused on \(\theta\), the unknown proportion of
students who would use the late-night bus. But the university might also
ask a more concrete question:

\begin{quote}
If \(1200\) students are on campus late during the exam period, how many
might use the bus?
\end{quote}

This is a prediction question.

A simple way to answer it is to use posterior simulation.

For each posterior draw of \(\theta\), we simulate a possible number of
bus users among \(1200\) students:

\[
\begin{aligned}
Y \mid \theta
&\sim \text{Binomial}(1200, \theta).
\end{aligned}
\]

This includes two sources of uncertainty:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  uncertainty about \(\theta\);
\item
  randomness in how many students actually use the service even if
  \(\theta\) were known.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2420}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{posterior\_prediction }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ posterior\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{predicted\_users =} \FunctionTok{rbinom}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(), }\AttributeTok{size =} \DecValTok{1200}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{prob =}\NormalTok{ theta)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{posterior\_prediction }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ predicted\_users, }\AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{after\_stat}\NormalTok{(density), }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Posterior predictive distribution"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{bins =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Predicted number of bus users out of 1200 students"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Posterior predictive distribution for bus usage"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{guides}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{color =} \FunctionTok{guide\_legend}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{override.aes =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{fill =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-19-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{posterior\_prediction }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Predictive mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(predicted\_users),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Predictive median}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{median}\NormalTok{(predicted\_users),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{2.5\% predictive quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(predicted\_users, }\FloatTok{0.025}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{97.5\% predictive quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(predicted\_users, }\FloatTok{0.975}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{P(more than 700 users)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(predicted\_users }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{700}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{everything}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Value"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{Value =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(Value, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.8\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lr}
\toprule
Summary & Value \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Predictive mean & 763.4 \\ 
Predictive median & 765.0 \\ 
2.5\% predictive quantile & 589.0 \\ 
97.5\% predictive quantile & 927.0 \\ 
P(more than 700 users) & 0.8 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

Posterior prediction is often more useful than parameter estimation
alone. Decision-makers usually care about observable consequences:
number of bus users, number of hospital beds needed, number of claims
next month, number of support calls, or number of customers who will
respond to an offer.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Parameter uncertainty and predictive uncertainty}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A posterior distribution describes uncertainty about an unknown
parameter.

A posterior predictive distribution describes uncertainty about future
or unobserved data.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{From posterior summaries to
decisions}\label{from-posterior-summaries-to-decisions}

A posterior distribution is not the end of an analysis. It is often the
beginning of a decision.

The university has to decide whether to run the late-night bus.

Suppose there are two possible actions:

\[
\begin{aligned}
a_1 &= \text{run the bus}, \\
a_0 &= \text{do not run the bus}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The best action depends on the consequences.

Suppose running the service costs \(\$18{,}000\) during the exam period.
Suppose each student trip is valued at \(\$20\) in safety, convenience,
and avoided transport costs.

If \(Y\) students use the bus, the net value of running it is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{net value}
&= 20Y - 18000.
\end{aligned}
\]

If the university does not run the bus, we will define the net value as
\(0\) for comparison.

Using the posterior predictive draws, we can simulate the net value of
running the service.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{bus\_decision }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ posterior\_prediction }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{net\_value\_run =} \DecValTok{20} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ predicted\_users }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{18000}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{better\_to\_run =}\NormalTok{ net\_value\_run }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{bus\_decision }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ net\_value\_run, }\AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{after\_stat}\NormalTok{(density), }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Net value if bus runs"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{bins =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_vline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{xintercept =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{linetype =} \StringTok{"dashed"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Net value of running the service"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Posterior distribution of the net value of running the bus"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Values above zero favour running the service"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{scale\_x\_continuous}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{labels =}\NormalTok{ dollar) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{guides}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{color =} \FunctionTok{guide\_legend}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{override.aes =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{fill =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-21-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{bus\_decision }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Expected net value if bus runs}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(net\_value\_run),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Median net value if bus runs}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{median}\NormalTok{(net\_value\_run),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{P(net value \textgreater{} 0)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(net\_value\_run }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{P(net value \textless{} {-}5000)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(net\_value\_run }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{5000}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{everything}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Value"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{Value =} \FunctionTok{if\_else}\NormalTok{(}
      \FunctionTok{str\_detect}\NormalTok{(Summary, }\StringTok{"P}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}}\StringTok{("}\NormalTok{),}
      \FunctionTok{as.character}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(Value, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)),}
      \FunctionTok{dollar}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(Value, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{90}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.9\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lr}
\toprule
Summary & Value \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Expected net value if bus runs & -\$2,732 \\ 
Median net value if bus runs & -\$2,700 \\ 
P(net value > 0) & 0.051 \\ 
P(net value < -5000) & 0.099 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

This is a different kind of output from a confidence interval or a
hypothesis test. We are using the posterior distribution to simulate
consequences under each possible action.

The decision still requires judgement. The value of a safe trip is not
an objective fact. The cost estimate may be uncertain. There may be
equity concerns, reputational concerns, or legal obligations that are
not captured by the simple calculation.

But the posterior simulation helps make the trade-off visible.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Bayesian analysis does not remove judgement}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

Bayesian inference can clarify uncertainty.

Decision analysis can clarify trade-offs.

But the final decision still depends on values, costs, consequences, and
context.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Loss functions}\label{loss-functions}

Decision theory gives a formal language for decisions under uncertainty.

A \textbf{loss function} describes how bad it is to take action \(a\)
when the true state of the world is \(\theta\).

We write this as:

\[
\begin{aligned}
L(\theta, a).
\end{aligned}
\]

In estimation problems, the action is usually a point estimate
\(\hat{\theta}\). Different loss functions lead to different best
estimates.

Two common loss functions are squared-error loss and absolute-error
loss.

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{1\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}llll}
\toprule
Loss function & Formula & Bayes estimate & Penalises large errors? \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Squared-error loss & L(theta, theta\_hat) = (theta - theta\_hat)\textasciicircum{}2 & Posterior mean & Strongly \\ 
Absolute-error loss & L(theta, theta\_hat) = |theta - theta\_hat| & Posterior median & Less strongly \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

Under squared-error loss:

\[
\begin{aligned}
L(\theta, \hat{\theta})
&= (\theta - \hat{\theta})^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

The Bayes estimator is the posterior mean:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\hat{\theta}_{\text{Bayes}}
&= E(\theta \mid X).
\end{aligned}
\]

Under absolute-error loss:

\[
\begin{aligned}
L(\theta, \hat{\theta})
&= |\theta - \hat{\theta}|.
\end{aligned}
\]

The Bayes estimator is the posterior median.

For symmetric posterior distributions, the mean and median may be
similar. For skewed posterior distributions, they can differ.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{skewed\_draws }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{theta =} \FunctionTok{rbeta}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{skewed\_summaries }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ skewed\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{mean =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{median =} \FunctionTok{median}\NormalTok{(theta)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{skewed\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{after\_stat}\NormalTok{(density), }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Posterior distribution"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{bins =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_vline}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ skewed\_summaries,}
    \FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{xintercept =}\NormalTok{ mean, }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Posterior mean"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{linetype =} \StringTok{"dashed"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_vline}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ skewed\_summaries,}
    \FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{xintercept =}\NormalTok{ median, }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Posterior median"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{linetype =} \StringTok{"dotted"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Different loss functions can lead to different point estimates"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{guides}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{color =} \FunctionTok{guide\_legend}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{override.aes =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{fill =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-24-1.pdf}}

The important lesson is not that one summary is always better. The
lesson is that a summary should match the purpose of the analysis.

If very large errors are especially bad, squared-error loss may be
natural. If errors are judged more linearly, absolute-error loss may be
more natural. If overestimation is more serious than underestimation, an
asymmetric loss function may be more appropriate.

\section{Asymmetric losses}\label{asymmetric-losses}

Some mistakes are not equally bad in both directions.

Suppose a hospital is estimating the number of beds needed during a
severe flu season. Overestimating demand may waste money.
Underestimating demand may leave patients without beds. These errors are
not equivalent.

A similar issue appears in the bus example. Underestimating demand may
leave students waiting at night. Overestimating demand may waste money
on an underused service.

One simple way to represent asymmetric loss is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
L(\theta, \hat{\theta})
&=
\begin{cases}
4(\theta - \hat{\theta}), & \text{if } \hat{\theta} < \theta, \\
\hat{\theta} - \theta, & \text{if } \hat{\theta} \geq \theta.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\]

This loss says that underestimating \(\theta\) is four times as costly
as overestimating it.

In this case, a higher posterior quantile may be a better estimate than
the posterior mean or median.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{asymmetric\_summary }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ posterior\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior median}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{median}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior 80\% quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(theta, }\FloatTok{0.80}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior 90\% quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(theta, }\FloatTok{0.90}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{everything}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Value"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{asymmetric\_summary }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{Value =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(Value, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.8\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lr}
\toprule
Summary & Value \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Posterior mean & 0.636 \\ 
Posterior median & 0.638 \\ 
Posterior 80\% quantile & 0.698 \\ 
Posterior 90\% quantile & 0.728 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The purpose of this section is not to memorise particular loss
functions. It is to recognise that estimation and decision-making are
connected.

\begin{quote}
A point estimate is not just a number. It is an action taken under
uncertainty.
\end{quote}

\section{Prior information as
pseudo-data}\label{prior-information-as-pseudo-data}

In Chapter 8, we used beta priors for proportions. One helpful
interpretation of a beta prior is that it acts like pseudo-data.

For a beta-binomial model:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\theta
&\sim \text{Beta}(\alpha, \beta), \\
X \mid \theta
&\sim \text{Binomial}(n, \theta), \\
\theta \mid X = x
&\sim \text{Beta}(\alpha + x,\ \beta + n - x).
\end{aligned}
\]

The prior hyperparameters \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) behave a little like
prior counts:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\alpha\) behaves like prior yes-counts;
\item
  \(\beta\) behaves like prior no-counts;
\item
  \(\alpha + \beta\) behaves like a prior sample size.
\end{itemize}

This interpretation is not perfect in every setting, but it is useful.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prior\_strength }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{prior =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Beta(1, 1)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Beta(2, 2)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Beta(10, 10)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Beta(40, 40)"}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{alpha =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{beta =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{prior\_mean =}\NormalTok{ alpha }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (alpha }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta),}
  \AttributeTok{prior\_sample\_size =}\NormalTok{ alpha }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta,}
  \AttributeTok{posterior\_alpha =}\NormalTok{ alpha }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ x,}
  \AttributeTok{posterior\_beta =}\NormalTok{ beta }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ x,}
  \AttributeTok{posterior\_mean =}\NormalTok{ posterior\_alpha }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (posterior\_alpha }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ posterior\_beta)}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{prior\_strength }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{prior\_mean =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(prior\_mean, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{posterior\_mean =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(posterior\_mean, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{Prior =}\NormalTok{ prior,}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Prior mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ prior\_mean,}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Prior pseudo{-}sample size}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ prior\_sample\_size,}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ posterior\_mean}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{1\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lrrr}
\toprule
Prior & Prior mean & Prior pseudo-sample size & Posterior mean \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Beta(1, 1) & 0.5 & 2 & 0.643 \\ 
Beta(2, 2) & 0.5 & 4 & 0.636 \\ 
Beta(10, 10) & 0.5 & 20 & 0.600 \\ 
Beta(40, 40) & 0.5 & 80 & 0.550 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

All four priors have the same prior mean of \(0.5\). But they do not
have the same strength. The stronger priors pull the posterior mean more
strongly toward \(0.5\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{prior\_strength\_density }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ prior\_strength }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{select}\NormalTok{(prior, posterior\_alpha, posterior\_beta) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{crossing}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{theta =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.999}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{density =} \FunctionTok{dbeta}\NormalTok{(theta, posterior\_alpha, posterior\_beta)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{prior\_strength\_density }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ theta, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ density, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ prior)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Posterior density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Prior"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Priors with the same centre can have different strengths"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Stronger priors pull the posterior more strongly toward the prior mean"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-27-1.pdf}}

This connects back to sensitivity analysis. If different reasonable
priors lead to very different posterior conclusions, the data may not be
strong enough to settle the question.

\section{A second story: estimating a
rate}\label{a-second-story-estimating-a-rate}

Not every Bayesian problem is about a proportion.

Suppose a regional clinic wants to estimate the average number of
after-hours calls it receives each night during winter. This matters
because staffing too few people can leave patients waiting, but staffing
too many people is costly.

Let \(X_i\) be the number of after-hours calls on night \(i\).

A simple model is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
X_i \mid \lambda
&\sim \text{Poisson}(\lambda),
\end{aligned}
\]

where \(\lambda\) is the average number of calls per night.

Suppose the clinic records calls over \(14\) nights:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{calls }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{22}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{26}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{24}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{21}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{17}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{25}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{23}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{27}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{22}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{24}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{call\_data }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{night =} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(calls),}
  \AttributeTok{calls =}\NormalTok{ calls}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{call\_data }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ night, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ calls, }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Observed calls"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{size =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Night"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Number of calls"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"After{-}hours calls over 14 winter nights"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-28-1.pdf}}

The sample mean is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(calls)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 21.92857
\end{verbatim}

Suppose previous winters suggest around \(20\) calls per night, but with
some uncertainty. A gamma prior is conjugate for the Poisson rate.

We write:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\lambda
&\sim \text{Gamma}(\alpha, \beta),
\end{aligned}
\]

where we use the shape-rate parameterisation. Under this
parameterisation:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E(\lambda)
&= \frac{\alpha}{\beta}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Let:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\lambda
&\sim \text{Gamma}(20, 1).
\end{aligned}
\]

This prior has mean \(20\). It acts roughly like prior information
equivalent to one night with \(20\) calls.

For Poisson data with a gamma prior, the posterior is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\lambda \mid x_1, \ldots, x_n
&\sim \text{Gamma}\left(\alpha + \sum_{i = 1}^{n}x_i,\ \beta + n\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{alpha\_lambda\_prior }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{20}
\NormalTok{beta\_lambda\_prior }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1}

\NormalTok{alpha\_lambda\_post }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ alpha\_lambda\_prior }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(calls)}
\NormalTok{beta\_lambda\_post }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ beta\_lambda\_prior }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(calls)}

\NormalTok{lambda\_draws }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{draw =} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{,}
  \AttributeTok{lambda =} \FunctionTok{rgamma}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{shape =}\NormalTok{ alpha\_lambda\_post, }\AttributeTok{rate =}\NormalTok{ beta\_lambda\_post)}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{lambda\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ lambda, }\AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{after\_stat}\NormalTok{(density), }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Posterior draws"}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_histogram}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{bins =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{fill =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_density}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(lambda),}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Density"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Posterior distribution for the average number of calls per night"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{guides}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{color =} \FunctionTok{guide\_legend}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{override.aes =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{fill =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{9_bayes2_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-30-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lambda\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(lambda),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior median}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{median}\NormalTok{(lambda),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{2.5\% quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(lambda, }\FloatTok{0.025}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{97.5\% quantile}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{quantile}\NormalTok{(lambda, }\FloatTok{0.975}\NormalTok{),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{P(lambda \textgreater{} 23)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(lambda }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{23}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{everything}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Value"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{Value =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(Value, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.8\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lr}
\toprule
Summary & Value \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Posterior mean & 21.79 \\ 
Posterior median & 21.77 \\ 
2.5\% quantile & 19.50 \\ 
97.5\% quantile & 24.16 \\ 
P(lambda > 23) & 0.16 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

Again, posterior simulation makes it easy to calculate the summaries we
care about.

If the clinic needs to decide how many staff to roster, the posterior
distribution of \(\lambda\) is useful. But an even more direct object is
the posterior predictive distribution for tomorrow night.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2420}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{call\_prediction }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ lambda\_draws }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}

\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
