\section{Credibility as a weighted
average}\label{credibility-as-a-weighted-average}

In conjugate Bayesian models, the posterior mean often looks like a
compromise between the data and the prior.

For the beta-binomial model, the posterior mean is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E(\theta \mid X = x)
&= \frac{\alpha + x}{\alpha + \beta + n}.
\end{aligned}
\]

This can be written as a weighted average of the sample proportion and
the prior mean:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E(\theta \mid X = x)
&= Z\left(\frac{x}{n}\right) + (1 - Z)\left(\frac{\alpha}{\alpha + \beta}\right),
\end{aligned}
\]

where:

\[
\begin{aligned}
Z
&= \frac{n}{\alpha + \beta + n}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The value \(Z\) is sometimes called a \textbf{credibility factor}. It
measures how much weight the posterior mean gives to the data-based
estimate.

For the bus example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{credibility }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Sample proportion}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ x }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ n,}
  \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Prior mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ alpha\_prior }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (alpha\_prior }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta\_prior),}
  \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Credibility factor Z}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ n }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (alpha\_prior }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta\_prior }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ n),}
  \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Posterior mean}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ alpha\_post }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (alpha\_post }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta\_post)}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{credibility }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{pivot\_longer}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{cols =} \FunctionTok{everything}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{names\_to =} \StringTok{"Quantity"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{values\_to =} \StringTok{"Value"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{Value =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(Value, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{gt}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{tab\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{table.width =} \FunctionTok{pct}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.8\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}lr}
\toprule
Quantity & Value \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
Sample proportion & 0.650 \\ 
Prior mean & 0.500 \\ 
Credibility factor Z & 0.909 \\ 
Posterior mean & 0.636 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The posterior mean is pulled toward the sample proportion because the
data contain more information than the weak prior.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Credibility factor}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A credibility factor is the weight given to the data-based estimate in a
posterior mean that can be written as a weighted average.

More data usually means a larger credibility factor.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Bayesian simulation as a
workflow}\label{bayesian-simulation-as-a-workflow}

The examples in this chapter all follow a similar pattern.

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{1\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}rll}
\toprule
Step & Bayesian workflow & Bus example \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
1 & State the question clearly & Would enough students use the late-night bus? \\ 
2 & Choose a probability model for the data & Survey yes-count follows a binomial distribution \\ 
3 & Choose a prior distribution & Use a beta prior for the unknown proportion \\ 
4 & Combine prior and likelihood to define the posterior & Posterior is proportional to prior times likelihood \\ 
5 & Simulate from the posterior & Draw plausible values of theta \\ 
6 & Summarise posterior and predictive uncertainty & Calculate credible intervals and P(theta > 0.60) \\ 
7 & Use the posterior to inform decisions & Compare the net value of running or not running the service \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The main conceptual shift is this:

\begin{quote}
We do not need to reduce the posterior to one number too quickly.
\end{quote}

A posterior distribution can be carried through the analysis. We can
transform posterior draws, use them to predict future observations, and
compare decisions.

\section{Recap}\label{recap-2}

In Chapter 8, we learned the logic of Bayesian updating:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{posterior}
&\propto \text{prior} \times \text{likelihood}.
\end{aligned}
\]

In this chapter, we learned how to work with the posterior once we have
it.

The key ideas were:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Posterior draws are simulated plausible parameter values.
\item
  Posterior summaries can be calculated directly from posterior draws.
\item
  Grid approximation uses prior times likelihood over many possible
  parameter values.
\item
  MCMC is useful when grid approximation is too inefficient.
\item
  Trace plots help us check whether an MCMC chain is exploring sensibly.
\item
  Posterior predictive distributions describe uncertainty about future
  or unobserved data.
\item
  Decisions can be compared by simulating consequences under the
  posterior.
\item
  Different loss functions can lead to different Bayesian estimates.
\end{itemize}

Bayesian inference is sometimes introduced as a formula. But in
practice, it is more like a workflow:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{prior information}
&\rightarrow \text{data} \\
&\rightarrow \text{posterior uncertainty} \\
&\rightarrow \text{prediction} \\
&\rightarrow \text{decision}.
\end{aligned}
\]

That final arrow matters. Statistical thinking is not only about
calculating uncertainty. It is also about using uncertainty carefully.

\section{Exercises}\label{exercises-8}

Short questions and longer applied questions are included below. Some
questions ask for calculations; others ask you to explain concepts in
words.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 1}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A small survey asks \(30\) students whether they would use a new weekend
study shuttle. Of the \(30\) students, \(18\) say yes.

Assume:

\[
\begin{aligned}
X \mid \theta
&\sim \text{Binomial}(30, \theta), \\
\theta
&\sim \text{Beta}(3, 3).
\end{aligned}
\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Find the posterior distribution for \(\theta\).
\item
  Calculate the posterior mean.
\item
  Interpret the posterior mean in context.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

The posterior is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\theta \mid X = 18
&\sim \text{Beta}(3 + 18,\ 3 + 30 - 18) \\
&\sim \text{Beta}(21,\ 15).
\end{aligned}
\]

The posterior mean is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E(\theta \mid X = 18)
&= \frac{21}{21 + 15} \\
&= \frac{21}{36} \\
&= 0.583.
\end{aligned}
\]

After combining the prior and the survey data, the estimated proportion
of students who would use the weekend shuttle is about \(0.583\), or
\(58.3\%\).
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 2}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

Explain the difference between a bootstrap distribution and a Bayesian
posterior distribution.

In your answer, describe what is being simulated in each case.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

A bootstrap distribution is created by resampling from the observed
data. It describes how a statistic might vary across repeated samples
similar to the one observed.

A Bayesian posterior distribution describes uncertainty about an unknown
parameter after combining prior information with the likelihood from the
observed data.

In the bootstrap, we simulate possible datasets or statistics. In
Bayesian posterior simulation, we simulate plausible parameter values.
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 3}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

Suppose you have \(5000\) posterior draws for a parameter \(\theta\). Of
these draws, \(3920\) are greater than \(0.7\).

Estimate:

\[
P(\theta > 0.7 \mid \text{data}).
\]

Interpret the result.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

The simulation estimate is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
P(\theta > 0.7 \mid \text{data})
&\approx \frac{3920}{5000} \\
&= 0.784.
\end{aligned}
\]

The interpretation is that, given the prior and the observed data, the
posterior probability that \(\theta\) is greater than \(0.7\) is
approximately \(0.784\).
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 4}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A researcher uses grid approximation for a proportion \(\theta\). They
create the following unnormalised posterior weights:

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.6\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}rr}
\toprule
theta & unnormalised\_weight \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
0.1 & 0.02 \\ 
0.2 & 0.10 \\ 
0.3 & 0.24 \\ 
0.4 & 0.12 \\ 
0.5 & 0.02 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Normalise the weights.
\item
  Which value of \(\theta\) has the highest posterior weight?
\item
  Estimate the posterior mean using this grid.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

The sum of the unnormalised weights is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
0.02 + 0.10 + 0.24 + 0.12 + 0.02
&= 0.50.
\end{aligned}
\]

The normalised weights are:

\begin{table}
\fontsize{12.0pt}{14.0pt}\selectfont
\begin{tabular*}{0.7\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}rrr}
\toprule
theta & unnormalised\_weight & posterior\_weight \\ 
\midrule\addlinespace[2.5pt]
0.1 & 0.02 & 0.04 \\ 
0.2 & 0.10 & 0.20 \\ 
0.3 & 0.24 & 0.48 \\ 
0.4 & 0.12 & 0.24 \\ 
0.5 & 0.02 & 0.04 \\ 
\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{table}

The highest posterior weight is at \(\theta = 0.3\).

The posterior mean estimate is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E(\theta \mid \text{data})
&\approx 0.1(0.04) + 0.2(0.20) + 0.3(0.48) + 0.4(0.24) + 0.5(0.04) \\
&= 0.004 + 0.040 + 0.144 + 0.096 + 0.020 \\
&= 0.304.
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 5}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A Metropolis chain is used to simulate from a posterior distribution.
The trace plot shows that the chain stays at exactly the same value for
long periods, with only occasional jumps.

What might be going wrong? What could be adjusted?

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

The proposal steps may be too large. If proposed values are usually in
low-posterior regions, they will often be rejected, causing the chain to
remain at the same value for long stretches.

One possible adjustment is to reduce the proposal standard deviation so
the chain proposes smaller, more acceptable moves.

However, proposal steps that are too small can also be a problem because
the chain may move too slowly. The goal is to find a proposal size that
allows the chain to explore the posterior without being rejected too
often.
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 6}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A clinic records the number of urgent phone calls received over \(10\)
evenings:

\[
14,\ 17,\ 15,\ 19,\ 18,\ 16,\ 20,\ 13,\ 17,\ 18.
\]

Assume:

\[
\begin{aligned}
X_i \mid \lambda
&\sim \text{Poisson}(\lambda), \\
\lambda
&\sim \text{Gamma}(12, 1),
\end{aligned}
\]

using the shape-rate parameterisation.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Find the posterior distribution for \(\lambda\).
\item
  Calculate the posterior mean.
\item
  Interpret the result.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

The total number of calls is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
14 + 17 + 15 + 19 + 18 + 16 + 20 + 13 + 17 + 18
&= 167.
\end{aligned}
\]

There are \(n = 10\) evenings.

For a gamma prior and Poisson likelihood:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\lambda \mid x_1, \ldots, x_n
&\sim \text{Gamma}\left(\alpha + \sum_{i = 1}^{n}x_i,\ \beta + n\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\lambda \mid \text{data}
&\sim \text{Gamma}(12 + 167,\ 1 + 10) \\
&\sim \text{Gamma}(179,\ 11).
\end{aligned}
\]

The posterior mean is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
E(\lambda \mid \text{data})
&= \frac{179}{11} \\
&= 16.27.
\end{aligned}
\]

After combining the prior with the observed call counts, the estimated
average number of urgent calls per evening is about \(16.27\).
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 7}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

Under squared-error loss, what posterior summary is used as the Bayes
estimator?

Under absolute-error loss, what posterior summary is used as the Bayes
estimator?

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

Under squared-error loss, the Bayes estimator is the posterior mean.

Under absolute-error loss, the Bayes estimator is the posterior median.

This shows that the best point estimate depends on how we define loss.
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 8}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A council is deciding whether to run a weekend community shuttle.

Posterior predictive simulation gives \(8000\) draws of the number of
weekly users. For each draw, the council calculates the net value of
running the service. The results are:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  mean net value: \(\$4200\);
\item
  median net value: \(\$3900\);
\item
  proportion of draws with net value above \(0\): \(0.87\);
\item
  proportion of draws with net value below \(-5000\): \(0.04\).
\end{itemize}

Write a short paragraph explaining what these results suggest.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

The posterior predictive simulation suggests that running the shuttle is
likely to have positive net value. The mean net value is \(\$4200\) and
the median is \(\$3900\), so the typical simulated outcome is
favourable. About \(87\%\) of simulated outcomes have net value above
zero, meaning the service is more likely than not to be beneficial under
the assumptions of the model. There is still some downside risk, with
about \(4\%\) of simulations producing a loss worse than \(\$5000\). A
reasonable conclusion is that the shuttle looks promising, but the
decision should also consider whether the council is comfortable with
the remaining downside risk and whether the cost and value assumptions
are credible.
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 9}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A posterior distribution is highly skewed to the right.

Would you expect the posterior mean and posterior median to be
identical? Which one would usually be larger?

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

They would usually not be identical.

For a distribution skewed to the right, the long right tail pulls the
mean upward. Therefore, the posterior mean would usually be larger than
the posterior median.
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomrule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Question 10}, breakable, bottomtitle=1mm, leftrule=.75mm, colback=white]

A student says:

\begin{quote}
MCMC is just a complicated version of the bootstrap.
\end{quote}

Explain why this statement is misleading.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Click for Solutions}

The statement is misleading because MCMC and the bootstrap simulate
different things for different purposes.

The bootstrap resamples from the observed data to approximate the
sampling distribution of a statistic. It is usually used to understand
how a statistic might vary across repeated samples.

MCMC constructs a dependent sequence of parameter values whose long-run
distribution approximates a posterior distribution. It is used in
Bayesian inference when the posterior is difficult to sample from
directly.

Both involve simulation, but they are not the same method and they
answer different questions.
\end{quote}

\end{tcolorbox}
